Jimmy Chin on His New Mount Everest Film and Latest Book

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Jimmy Chin, pictured in Joshua Tree National Park, described writing his new book as both a marathon and a sprint. (Photo: Beau Grealy/Trunk Archive)

In spring 2026, Jimmy Chin flew to Nepal. It was his 15th trip to the Greater Ranges over 25 years spent on expeditions to climb and photograph its snow-covered giants. The trip was a departure from the high-octane sufferfests he’s often done, and it also marked a different type of first: the first time he brought his kids, Marina (12) and James (10), along. Showing them the mountains that have inspired him for decades, and often pulled him away from them for months at a time, was memorable for Chin.

Together, they made their way to Everest Base Camp, stopping in the tiny villages that populate the roadless Khumbu Valley. When they crawled into their tents at 17,598 feet, they were proud of themselves.

Outside Phortse, a small village in Sagarmatha National Park, a helicopter dropped off a special guest who’d come to say hi: Jim Morrison. Morrison hadn’t wanted to miss the kids’ first trip to Nepal, and they were shocked to see him walking toward them on the trail. In town, they went to the Khumbu Climbing Center, where Nepali climbers and high-altitude workers train to operate safely in the big mountains, to hang with Sherpa friends and their families, and to pick up their guide, Pemba Sherpa (Sharwa).

Twenty years earlier, Chin had encountered a then 15-year-old Pemba for the first time in that exact spot. It was just a few years after the center had opened, and he and the legendary mountaineer Conrad Anker were on the way to climb Cholatse. “I really encouraged his photography and filmmaking,” said Chin.

It turned out to be kismet: Pemba worked with Chin as a cinematographer on his film, Everest: The Other Side. Pemba was there in October 2025 documenting Jim Morrison as he made his first turn off the 29,032-foot summit. Morrison made history that day after completing a mind-boggling ski descent: an unforgiving 9,000-foot line of the North Face of Everest via the Hornbein and Japanese Couloirs.

Chin’s team of climbers, filmmakers, and support crew added 12 new names to the short list of people who had ever summited Everest via the North Face. Ascending the route alone would’ve been enough, but Chin was there as Morrison’s climbing partner while simultaneously directing a feature film. The 53-year-old has made a career of combining high-angle endeavors with filmmaking. In 2011, he climbed the Shark’s Fin, a 4,500-foot route, alongside Anker and Renan Ozturk to film what would become the 2015 movie Meru. When Alex Honnold climbed the 3,000-foot granite prow of El Capitan in Yosemite National Park without a rope, Chin was there to capture that too. Free Solo went on to win an Academy Award.

His newest film, which was co-directed with Elizabeth Chai Vasarhelyi and made its theatrical debut in IMAX on October 30, was built on every single one of the skills Chin has developed over his career climbing and documenting expeditions on some of the world’s most challenging peaks.

“This is kind of like the culmination of all my collective experiences as a climber and a photographer and a filmmaker and a producer and a director and all the things,” said Chin of making the film.

When Chin took the last few steps to the summit of Mount Everest, his third time standing up there, he was surprised to find himself overcome with emotion. During the climb, he’d been so focused: He was thinking about breathing, consumed by the sheer physicality of climbing at altitude, busy worrying about Morrison and the team’s safety, and planning out shot lists and contingency plans. He’d thought about every worst-case scenario, but he’d failed to consider what it would feel like to succeed. The years of hard work flashed in front of his eyes. He collapsed into Morrison’s arms, in tears.

“I looked over into Nepal and saw Makalu and Lhotse, and I mean the whole Himalaya spread out in front of me,” said Chin. It had taken seven years to get the team up there. “Everything hit me at the same time and I totally fell apart.”

While he felt a big weight lift from his shoulders after getting everyone up and down Everest safely, he still felt the enormous weight of having two of his closest friends, Morrison and Morrison’s late partner Hilaree Nelson, entrust him with their story. He’d met Nelson 20 years earlier when they both joined the athlete team at The North Face at the same time. He knew he had to get it right.

After six years of working on the film, Chin and producer Shannon Dill traveled to Tahoe City last June to show Morrison a rough cut. When it was over, there was a lot of emotion in the room, and people were crying. Morrison got up and walked to the back of the theater to wrap Chin in a big hug.

During the final phases of editing the film, Chin was also working on a deadline to finish his new book, Rules to Live By. “I had like three months of 16-hour days, seven days a week,” he said. “When people say it’s a marathon, not a sprint, I was like, ‘This is both.’”

For the book, Chin interviewed ten people he looks up to, from Lynsey Addario to Simone Biles to Yo-Yo Ma, all of whom have helped shape the way he thinks about what really matters in life.

Both Bill King and Ridley Scott talked to him about honesty, and it’s stuck with him. “You have to tell the truth,” he said. “It’s hard to do, but you have to do it, and you have to do it early because it only becomes harder.” Telling the truth is like training a muscle, he said, and exercising it becomes easier with practice and, with enough time, just becomes how you naturally operate. We’ve all had an experience that would’ve been easier if we’d just been honest up front, said Chin.

He draws inspiration from actress Annette Bening and how she leads: quietly, in a way that embodies the Daoist idea that the greatest leaders are the ones that no one knows are leading. “She makes people feel seen and heard, and when she walks on set, people will do anything for this woman because they want to,” he said. “I was really paying attention to how she moved through the world. It’s about listening to people and making people feel heard.”

Years after Pemba met Chin, he started working on climbing expeditions, following in his father’s footsteps. His dad bought him a Lumix digital camera to take with him on his first expedition to Mount Everest. On the summit, he snapped a few photos.

When Meru came out, Pemba remembers feeling deeply inspired by Chin’s work. He could not have imagined that a decade later, after years spent working in the mountains and honing his craft, he would find himself filming for Chin’s production, with his camera aimed at Chin during the emotional moments that transpired on the summit before Morrison’s ski.

Chin has spent much of his life forging his own path in the mountains, making daring first ascents and ski descents, but has since shifted his focus toward giving opportunities to the next generation coming up behind him. When a big objective is on the horizon, he makes sure to bring new faces along.

“Jimmy is a big influence on me, and a big mentor,” said Pemba. “Experiencing a climb with the man himself and working for the man himself? Yeah, it was really an honor.”

178 Websites Infected in Major Optimizely Hack: Crypto Wallets Targeted, but Bitcoin Barely Reacted

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Optimizely attack infects 178 websites, steals crypto and card data, and exposes users to wallet-address swaps and iPhone exploits.

An Optimizely attack on Monday, November 16, planted crypto-stealing code on the websites of 178 organizations, including Ledger, HSBC, Zoom, the BBC and CNN. The malicious script swapped crypto wallet addresses, skimmed card numbers, and tried to break into iPhones for about 19 hours before it was cleaned up.

BREAKING: A cyberattack hit 178 major organizations, including Zoom, CNN, BBC, Fox News, HSBC, Ledger, Equifax, Salesforce, IKEA and Air France-KLM, by loading malware on the Optimizely Web platform, exposing visitors to scripts built to steal payment data and exploit iOS…

— The Hormuz Letter (@HormuzLetter) November 17, 2026

Optimizely is a tool companies use to test different versions of their web pages. An attacker slipped code into the scripts it serves, so every site that loaded them ran the malware.

We traced the attacker’s Bitcoin wallet. It received five payments worth about $5,500, and one victim alone sent nearly 90% of it. Bitcoin barely moved while the attack ran.

So what happened, and what is the real impact of hacks like this one?

Security researcher Scott Helme of Report URI found the code and published a full breakdown. The attacker wrote it into a settings field in customers’ Optimizely projects, so it was served from Optimizely’s own servers and looked legitimate.

Optimizely said it “secured affected user accounts and added protections against further unauthorized code delivery.” Report URI found no sign that Ledger hardware wallets themselves were breached. The danger was on the website, where a copied address could be swapped before a user pasted it.

Market Intelligence: Crypto Security Analyst Recommends Best Anonymous Crypto Wallets

Report URI listed the attacker’s wallets. We checked the Bitcoin one on mempool.space, the public Bitcoin block explorer.

The Optimizely attack’s impact was tiny, and the market ignored it. Bitcoin is near $64,300 after its weekend low of $62,006, and that slide came from wider selling, not this attack.

EXPLORE: Crypto Hack Losses Hit $8.74 Billion in 2026, More Than Triple 2025: How Did Bitcoin and Ether React?

The money stolen in the Optimizely attack is small, but the method is the year’s most damaging one. We sorted DefiLlama’s 2026 hack list by attack type. Attacks on websites and infrastructure, rather than on smart contracts, are now the costliest category:

These attacks hit users where no wallet setting can protect them. The fix is on the user’s side: check the address on the hardware wallet’s screen before signing, send a small test transfer first, and keep phones updated.

On the daily chart, Bitcoin trades at $64,326. It sits below its 50-day exponential moving average (EMA), an average that gives recent prices more weight, at $76,208, and its 200-day EMA at $75,864.

The Optimizely attack did not move Bitcoin, and the attacker walked away with about $7,000. Its real cost is trust: when a hardware-wallet maker’s own website can serve malware for hours, checking every address on the device is no longer optional.

I built a tool to find people asking for my product. Most of the work was throwing results away.

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

For my last product I sent about 2,000 cold emails. Almost nobody replied. What bothered me more was what I found afterwards: the people who needed it had been asking for it in public the whole time. In forums, in review sites, in "what tool do you use for X" threads.

So I started building Crossroad (https://crossroadhq.com/?utm_source=devto). The idea is simple: find people and companies who are publicly asking for what you sell, show you exactly what they said, and help you write a reply you send yourself.

The idea is simple. Doing it honestly is not. Here is what I learned building the first version.

1. Searching is easy. Deciding is the product.

Step 2 returns a lot. Step 3 is where almost everything gets thrown away.

In a real test week for a small CRM for independent insurance agencies, the tool read 48 results and kept 2. Both were business news: an agency opening a new office, and an agency acquiring another one. Those are moments when an agency suddenly needs better tooling. The other 46 were set aside, each with a reason.

2. Most of the open web is vendors talking

For broad products (a helpdesk, a developer tool, a generic AI assistant) the open web is mostly vendor content: comparison pages, "top 10 tools" lists, launch posts. They contain all the right keywords and zero buyers.

Keyword matching can't tell the difference. "Looking for a CRM for my agency" and "We built the best CRM for agencies" share almost every word. So every result gets a question: is this a person or company with the need, or someone selling to them? Vendor content gets dropped, however well it matches.

The honest consequence: the tool works best for products with a clear type of buyer. For very broad products I'd rather show nothing than show noise.

If any of that is missing, it isn't shown. A summary of "why they fit" without the original text is just a guess you can't check.

4. Finding isn't the bottleneck. Replying is.

This week another founder building something similar shared his numbers on Reddit: his users contacted about 2% of the people the tool found. Finding people wasn't the problem. "I don't know what to say to a stranger, so I'll do it tomorrow" was.

So the reply matters more than the list. A good reply helps with the actual question first, and only mentions the product if it's genuinely the answer. And the tool never posts for you. Communities are right to ban bots, and an automated reply is spam no matter how well it's targeted.

The site went live two days ago and early access is open. The first three matches for each product are free, checked by hand while I learn what makes people actually reply.

If you've ever found customers by answering questions in public, I'd love to hear what made you hit send.

DHCP Client Identifier vs MAC Address: What a Router Is Showing

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Posted on Nov 17 • Originally published at deviceshelf.app

A router's DHCP page may show a local IP address, a hardware or MAC address, a hostname, and sometimes a field called “client ID” or “DHCP client identifier.” When two entries look similar, it's easy to read client ID as another name for the MAC address. That isn't always the case.

The difference matters when you're checking a changed router entry or deciding whether to create a DHCP reservation. A router can associate a lease with information supplied by the client, but router interfaces don't all display that information the same way. It also can't reliably tell you which person or physical device is behind an entry. The task is more specific: work out which current lease belongs to a device you manage before changing anything.

DHCP gives a client configuration details such as an IP address, subnet mask, router address, and lease time. A client request can include a client identifier option. DHCP messages also carry a hardware-address field. RFC 2131 defines these parts of the exchange.

Not every router shows both values, though, and the labels aren't always accurate. One interface may show only “MAC address.” Another may put “client ID” next to a separate MAC address. A third may get its device label from a remembered lease. Before comparing two rows, write down the exact field labels and local IP address, along with the displayed lease time and whether the row is active or historical. Only do this on a network you own or are authorized to manage.

Don't guess at missing details. A field that looks like a MAC address could be a hardware address, an identifier built from one, or a vendor-specific way of displaying the information. Check the documentation for that router model to find out what a particular screen means. The DHCP specifications explain the protocol's terms; they don't determine what a consumer router calls a column.

A MAC address is a link-layer address used to deliver traffic on a local network. On an IPv4 segment, Address Resolution Protocol associates an IP address with a link-layer address for local delivery. That's the limited role described in RFC 826. It doesn't make a MAC address a permanent, universal identity for an entire device.

A DHCP client identifier is an option carried in DHCP messages that lets a DHCP server recognize a client for lease management. Some clients use an identifier related to a hardware address. Others can use a different identifier. RFC 4361 specifies a DHCPv4 client-identifier format that combines an identity association identifier (IAID) with a DHCP unique identifier (DUID), also used by DHCPv6. So a client identifier and a displayed Wi-Fi MAC address can be related without being identical text.

Devices with more than one interface make this distinction particularly useful. A laptop may have both Ethernet and Wi-Fi interfaces, while a phone can use a network-specific private Wi-Fi address. A virtual machine, dock, or USB adapter can introduce another interface. If the router records a lease for each interface, several entries may belong to equipment you recognize. But similar names alone don't establish that two records belong to the same device.

Changing a reservation or reconnecting can interrupt services and your remote session. Make these changes with local access or an alternative management connection available.

If you have physical or administrative access to the device you think an entry belongs to, compare its current settings instead of relying on an old list. Connect it to the relevant network and open its network settings. Note the local IPv4 address and the current MAC address reported for that interface. Then find the router's active lease with the same local IP and compare its time and connection type, where available.

Don't assign a duplicate IP to create an address conflict as a test. It can interrupt another device and make the original evidence harder to interpret. A normal renewal, reconnect, or documented router refresh is a safer way to see which current lease changes.

DHCP data is meant for configuration, not proof of ownership. A router may keep an expired or remembered entry after a device disconnects. When that device reconnects, it may use another interface or receive a different address. It may also supply a different identifier after an operating-system change. A Wi-Fi privacy setting can cause a familiar device to present a network-specific address, too.

Privacy is one reason not to require a stable, human-readable identifier from every DHCP client. RFC 7844 discusses DHCP identifiers and options that can make a client easier to correlate. It won't explain an individual router entry. It does explain why a changing or limited identifier can be deliberate protocol behavior rather than a sign of a network fault.

Only draw conclusions the evidence supports. Example observation: “This active lease matched the laptop's current Wi-Fi address at 14:20” is a useful observation. “This identifier proves who owns the device” is not. If you still can't explain a record, mark it as pending. Before changing access rules or passwords, check nearby facts such as a guest network, a recently added adapter, or a device that's asleep.

A DHCP reservation is useful when a managed service needs a predictable local address, such as a NAS share, printer queue, or home-automation endpoint. Use whichever identifier the router explicitly asks for. That means a MAC address on some routers; on others, it may mean a documented client-ID value. Before copying an identifier from an old historical row, confirm that the current device still uses it.

Once you've saved a reservation, renew the lease only on the device you manage and check that it receives the expected address. Test the intended local service from another authorized device. A reservation doesn't guarantee a fixed hostname or continuous availability. You still need a short inventory note explaining why it exists.

For another local observation, an inventory tool can help organize the IP address, interface address, time, and service clues seen on a network you administer. DeviceShelf is one option for that comparison. As with the router record, compare its result with the device's own settings. It isn't a guarantee of identity or security.

Pause if you can't match a current active lease to any authorized device after checking the network name, connection type, timing, and known adapters. Review who has access to the Wi-Fi password and whether a guest network is separate. Check for pending router firmware updates as well. Avoid publishing a complete DHCP table or screenshot, since it can expose local addresses, hardware addresses, and device names.

Changing a Wi-Fi password can be appropriate when you have a grounded reason to suspect unauthorized access. It's disruptive, though, and a client-identifier mismatch alone usually isn't enough. First, keep a record of what the router shows and compare the current device settings you can access. Then make the smallest safe change that answers the next question.

China engineers behind J-35 fighter jet pioneer 3D-printed load-bearing structures in military aircraft

China has been at this for over two decades, and they're not slowing down.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

The Shenyang Aircraft Design and Research Institute, the developers of China’s J-35 fighter jet platform, used metal 3D printing to create large primary load-bearing structures for military aircraft applications. Global Times reports the structure is over 16.4 feet (five meters) in length, using segmented printing and fusion techniques.

Shenyang Aircraft Design and Research Institute, a research organization under the Aviation Industry Corporation of China (AVIC), has been working on 3D-printed aircraft components since 2003. The impressively large 3D-printed components are the culmination of decades of research and development of various metal additive manufacturing techniques.

The engineers are no strangers to large 3D-printed components: in 2005, they achieved secondary load-bearing structures; then, in 2012, large primary load-bearing titanium alloy structures, earning additive manufacturing expert Wang Huaming and his team China’s National Technological Invention Award.

Recently, however, the team created a primary load-bearing structure even larger, at over 16.4 feet. The massive component is not printed in one piece, as printing such a large structure faces warping and cracking issues, as stated in Progress and Challenges of Laser Direct Manufacturing of Large Titanium Structural Components, published in the Chinese Journal of Lasers. To avoid these problems, two techniques were employed: Segmented Additive Manufacturing (SAM) and additive fusion joining. It sounds like fancy words to say it was printed separately and welded, and it technically wouldn’t be wrong — but the key distinction is that it is part of the whole workflow; basically, metal 3D printing into the intentionally designed gaps between the components and creating one whole printed piece.

Additive manufacturing eliminates the need for expensive molds and multi-part construction. Titanium alloys are notoriously difficult to machine, cast components are usually weak, and joining parts using bolts and screws is additional labor — metal 3D printing solves all of this by creating a strong printed part in the desired shape in one large piece, minimizing the need for additional fasteners. By eliminating molds, the team can iterate much faster and create more complex structures that would otherwise not be possible with traditional manufacturing techniques.

10 rules and cases that explain the Grammys' Best New Artist category

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Free daily briefing on global business news.

Grammy Best New Artist eligibility rests on release limits and a judgment call about breakthrough, and these 10 rules and cases show how established acts still count as new

Chappell Roan released her debut single, "Good Hurt," in 2017. At the ceremony held in February 2025, she won the Grammy for Best New Artist. Those two facts show why the award confuses people.

The word "new" in the category name does not mean a first release. It does not mean a first year of making music either. The Recording Academy uses the category to recognize a breakthrough, and a breakthrough can arrive long after a debut.

Grammy Best New Artist eligibility rests on a two-part test. The first part is numerical. The Academy's published guidance says a candidate must have released at least five singles or tracks, or one album. The same guidance caps eligibility at no more than 30 singles or tracks, or three albums. Those limits leave a wide band between a brand-new act and an established one.

The second part depends on judgment. A candidate must achieve what the Academy calls a "breakthrough into the public consciousness" and have an impact on the musical landscape during the eligibility year. No chart position or sales figure defines that standard. The Academy's review process decides who meets it, and it also settles eligibility and category placement more broadly.

That mix of hard limits and soft judgment drives the confusion around the category. An artist with several albums can still qualify if the breakthrough falls in the right year. An artist with a long history can still be treated as new.

The category also has a fixed place in the Grammys' structure. It belongs to the General Field, the same group that includes Record of the Year, Album of the Year and Song of the Year.

The 10 slides that follow look at the rules first and then at specific cases. They cover the release floor and ceiling, the breakthrough standard and the gap between a career start and a breakthrough. They also cover winners and nominees who show how the rules play out, from the 2025 field to Milli Vanilli's revoked 1990 award. This is a selection of rules and cases, not a complete history of the award.

A Best New Artist candidate must have released at least five singles or tracks, or one album, under the Recording Academy's published guidance. This floor is the first hard number in the category's eligibility test.

The rule offers two separate routes past the bar. One is a run of individual releases that reaches five singles or tracks. The other is a single album. An act needs to meet only one of the two.

The two routes suit different release habits. An artist who puts out songs one at a time can clear the floor without ever releasing an album. An artist whose first project is a full album clears it with that single release.

The floor measures recorded output, not reach. An artist with fewer than five singles or tracks and no album falls below it. A large audience for those few releases does not change the count.

The minimum works as a gate rather than a qualification on its own. Clearing it does not make an artist a contender. The Academy also requires a breakthrough into the public consciousness and an impact on the musical landscape during the eligibility year. That part is assessed by judgment rather than by a chart or sales threshold.

Because the floor is defined by a count, it can be checked directly against an artist's discography. The breakthrough test offers no equivalent figure to check. Whether an entry meets the rules, and where it is placed among the categories, is decided through the Academy's review process.

The minimum is not tied to an artist's first release or first year of making music. An act that crossed the five-track or one-album line years earlier is not excluded for that reason alone. A separate ceiling on total output sets the limit at the other end of the scale.

The Recording Academy's guidance sets a ceiling for Best New Artist. A candidate can have released no more than 30 singles or tracks, or three albums. An act whose catalog has grown beyond that point falls outside the category's release limits.

The cap is one side of a two-sided release test. A separate minimum sets the floor. Between the two lies a band of output, and an act has to sit inside it before the Academy weighs anything else.

The ceiling sits higher than the word "new" suggests. Three albums means more than one complete record already in circulation. Thirty tracks is more material than a single album normally holds. An artist close to the limit already has a substantial body of recorded work.

The cap counts releases, not years. It measures how much music an artist has put out, not how long the artist has been active. A musician who records slowly can spend a long time in the industry and still stay under the limit. That is one reason a performer with a long career can still appear in the category.

Staying under the ceiling is necessary but not sufficient. The Academy also looks for a breakthrough and an impact on the musical landscape during the eligibility year. Both are assessed by judgment. The Academy's review process decides whether an entry is eligible and where it belongs.

The 2025 field shows how far the category can reach into established careers. Sabrina Carpenter was a nominee at the 2025 ceremony after releasing several albums before that year. Her place in the field shows that a sizable recording history does not by itself end an artist's claim to being new in the Academy's sense.

For anyone trying to predict a Best New Artist field, the cap answers one narrow question. It rules out acts whose output has already passed the limit. It says nothing about whether a qualifying act has made enough of a mark to earn a nomination. That second question is left to the Academy's judgment.

The heart of Grammy Best New Artist eligibility is a judgment call. The Recording Academy's guidance asks whether a candidate achieved a "breakthrough into the public consciousness" and made an impact on the musical landscape during the eligibility year.

That wording carries most of the weight in the category. The release limits on either side of it are fixed counts. The breakthrough test is phrased in words instead.

The Academy does not tie the test to a chart position or a sales figure. No commercial benchmark settles the question automatically.

That gap is the main source of confusion around the category. The word "new" suggests a statement about when an artist started. The Academy's language points elsewhere. It asks when the public began paying attention, and whether that moment landed inside the eligibility year.

Two phrases in the guidance do separate work. "Public consciousness" reads as a question of awareness: whether listeners beyond an existing following came to know the artist. "Impact on the musical landscape" reads as a question of influence on music itself during that period.

A qualitative standard has to be applied case by case. The Academy's review process handles that task, deciding which acts are eligible and where submissions are placed. Each eligibility year brings a new set of candidates to weigh against the same wording.

Timing matters as much as the breakthrough itself. A rise that lands before or after the eligibility year does not meet the test as written. That makes the date of an artist's breakthrough, rather than the date of a first release, the deciding question.

The release limits work differently. Whether an act falls inside them can be settled by counting its singles, tracks and albums. The breakthrough test adds a second layer that only the Academy's reading of an artist's year can resolve.

In practice, eligibility for this award cannot be checked against a public scoreboard.

A long career does not rule an artist out of Best New Artist. The Recording Academy does not tie eligibility to a first release or to the year an act started making music. What matters is whether the artist's breakthrough falls within the eligibility period.

The category's name suggests otherwise. That gap between the label and the rule is where much of the confusion starts. In everyday speech, "new" implies a debut. At the Grammys, it marks the moment an artist reaches a wide audience, not the moment that artist first put music out.

Two separate timelines are in play. One is the career itself: the first recordings, the early releases and the years of building a following. The other is the breakthrough, when the artist moves into the public consciousness and makes an impact on the musical landscape. Only the second timeline decides whether an act counts as new.

An artist can spend years releasing music to a modest audience and still become eligible later. If the wider breakthrough arrives during the eligibility period, the earlier work does not cancel it out. The length of a career is not a mark against an act by itself.

A long history still has limits. The Academy's guidance caps the number of singles, tracks and albums an eligible artist can have released. A veteran act qualifies only if its catalog stays under those caps and its breakthrough meets the Academy's standard.

The decision does not rest on an artist's own claim to be new. The Academy's review process determines which entries are eligible and where they are placed. That review, rather than a fixed debut date, settles whether an act with an earlier history moves forward.

Best New Artist belongs to the General Field, alongside Record of the Year, Album of the Year and Song of the Year. An act with a long career that clears the test competes in that same field as a new artist.

For listeners, the useful question is about timing rather than biography. An artist with years of releases behind them can still be a valid Best New Artist contender. The test is when the public caught on, not when the music began.

Chappell Roan won Best New Artist at the Grammy ceremony held in February 2025. Her debut single, "Good Hurt," had come out in 2017, eight years before that win.

Her record shows how far the Recording Academy's idea of "new" can stretch. By the time she collected the award, she had been releasing music for most of a decade. The category did not treat that history as a barrier.

Her timeline has three fixed points. The first is "Good Hurt" in 2017. The second is her debut album, The Rise and Fall of a Midwest Princess, which came out in 2023. The third is the February 2025 ceremony.

The spacing between those points matters. Six years separated the debut single from the debut album. Another two years separated the album from the win. A reader who looks only at the first date would expect a veteran act, not a new one.

The album also settles one side of the numerical test. Under the Academy's guidance, a single album is enough on its own to meet the minimum release requirement. A first album also leaves room below the ceiling the guidance sets on albums.

Meeting the release limits does not finish the assessment. The Academy also asks whether a candidate broke into the public consciousness and made an impact on the musical landscape during the eligibility year. That part of the test rests on judgment rather than a chart position or a sales total.

Her win shows how the two parts of the test interact. The album answered the numerical question. The judgment question turned on the period the award covered, not on the age of her career.

For anyone looking at future Best New Artist fields, her timeline offers a practical marker. A debut single several years before a nomination does not rule an artist out. An earlier album does not rule an artist out either, provided the catalog stays under the release cap.

Sabrina Carpenter was one of eight Best New Artist nominees at the 2025 ceremony, even though she had released several albums before that year. Her nomination shows that an existing catalog does not, by itself, keep an artist out of the category.

The full 2025 field was Benson Boone, Sabrina Carpenter, Chappell Roan, Doechii, Khruangbin, Raye, Shaboozey and Teddy Swims. Roan took the award.

The list put artists at different points in their careers on the same ballot. That mix is built into how the category works.

Carpenter's recorded output makes the point clear. The category's name suggests a debut act. Her discography pointed the other way, with several albums released before the ceremony that recognized her.

What put her in the running was the timing of her breakthrough, not the age of her career. The question was whether her wider public impact fell inside the period the 2025 ceremony covered. Her nomination reflects a judgment that it did.

The same lens applied to the rest of the field. No nominee was measured against a fixed number of years in the industry. Each was assessed on whether a breakthrough landed within the period the ceremony covered.

That is how a longer catalog and a shorter one could share a ballot under the same rules.

The 2025 list also offers a practical guide for reading future nominations. A familiar name with past albums is not automatically ruled out. A newer name is not automatically ruled in. Each case turns on when the artist's wider audience arrived, and on the Academy's view of that timing.

Jazz bassist and singer Esperanza Spalding won Best New Artist at the 2011 Grammy ceremony. The other nominees that year were Justin Bieber, Drake, Florence + the Machine and Mumford & Sons.

The result is often cited as evidence that the category does not follow popularity alone. That reading rests on the contrast between the winner and the rest of the field.

Every act on that list had cleared the same eligibility screen before the vote. The Recording Academy's review process decides who can compete in the category. Its guidance sets one minimum and one maximum for released music, and those limits apply to every candidate regardless of genre. Spalding, Bieber, Drake, Florence + the Machine and Mumford & Sons all fell inside those bounds.

Meeting those limits only puts an artist in contention. The deciding standard is whether the artist achieved a breakthrough into the public consciousness during the eligibility year. The Academy also looks for an impact on the musical landscape in that same period.

Neither standard is measured by a chart position or a sales total. Both require judgment. The same body of work can be read in more than one way. That leaves room for an outcome that differs from a ranking by audience size.

Best New Artist belongs to the Grammys' General Field, which is not tied to any one genre. Genre categories elsewhere in the Grammys group artists by musical style. Best New Artist does not.

That open format put Spalding on the same ballot as Bieber and Drake in 2011. A jazz bassist, a pop singer and a rapper were judged against one another under a single standard. Two bands completed the field.

The 2011 result also shows why the category is hard to forecast from fan bases alone. Voters weigh breakthrough and impact, not only popularity. Their verdict can differ from the one the charts would suggest.

Chance the Rapper won Best New Artist at the 2017 ceremony. He built his audience by giving his mixtapes away for free and reaching listeners through streaming.

His case shows the breakthrough standard applied to an artist who did not sell his music in the usual way. The Recording Academy asks whether a candidate broke into the public consciousness during the eligibility year. It also asks whether the artist made an impact on the musical landscape in that same period.

Neither question is framed in terms of revenue. The Academy answers both by judgment, without a chart position or sales total as a cutoff. That left room for an artist whose listeners found him through free downloads and streams rather than purchases. A free release can still reach a large audience, and the test looks at reach and impact, not at money changing hands.

The free-release route also raises a practical question about the numerical rules. Best New Artist candidates must clear a minimum release count and stay under a ceiling. How a free mixtape fits that count is a matter for the Academy's review process, which settles eligibility and category placement.

His night produced more than one trophy. Chance the Rapper also won Best Rap Album at the same ceremony. That paired a win in a genre category with a win in the General Field, the group that holds Best New Artist.

The two awards measured different things. Best Rap Album honored a body of recorded work within one genre. Best New Artist honored the arrival of the artist across all of music.

His win also separates two ideas that the category's name tends to blur. Putting out music is one event, and breaking through is another. Chance the Rapper had released free projects before the ceremony that honored him. The award marked when his audience arrived, not when his first release did.

Milli Vanilli won Best New Artist in 1990 and lost the award later that same year. The Recording Academy revoked it in November 1990 after it emerged that the duo had not sung on their album.

It remains the best-known rescinded award in the category.

Most arguments about Best New Artist concern timing. Listeners ask whether an act's catalog is too large, or whether a breakthrough happened in the right year. Others ask whether the breakthrough was real at all. The Milli Vanilli case raised a different question. It asked whether the winners had performed the vocals on the record that made them famous.

The eligibility rules do not settle that question. Release limits measure how much music an act has put out. The breakthrough standard measures public impact during the eligibility year. Neither test, by itself, confirms who sang on a recording.

By winning, the duo had cleared the breakthrough standard. The problem was what that rise rested on. The album behind it carried vocals the duo had not sung.

The timing of the decision sets the case apart. The revocation did not come before the ceremony. It came after voting had ended and the award had been presented. That sequence shows a Best New Artist win can be reopened after the fact.

The case also clarifies what the category honors. Best New Artist goes to an artist, not to a single record or song. An act's claim to be new rests on its own work reaching the public. The 1990 revocation tested that link between the credited act and the music.

The case marks an outer boundary for the category. Most debates over who counts as new turn on judgment about timing and impact. The Milli Vanilli decision turned on a fact about who performed the music. The Academy treated that fact as grounds to take the award back.

Billie Eilish won Best New Artist at the 2020 ceremony, and Olivia Rodrigo won it at the 2022 ceremony. Both had released music earlier. In each case, the breakthrough came in the year the award covered.

Their wins came at separate ceremonies, but they follow the same logic. For both, the Recording Academy looked past an artist's first release. It focused on the point when her music broke through.

The category does not ask when someone started recording. It asks when the wider public started paying attention. It also asks whether that attention changed the musical landscape during the eligibility year.

Eilish and Rodrigo each had a recording history before the year that mattered. That history did not count against them. Songs released ahead of a breakthrough can stay in an artist's catalog without closing off the award, as long as the total stays within the Academy's release ceiling.

Earlier releases can also work in a candidate's favor. The Academy sets a minimum amount of released music for consideration. An artist who puts out songs for some time before a breakout is likely to have cleared that floor by the time the public notices.

The harder question is timing. The Academy judges a breakthrough into the public consciousness rather than measuring it with a chart position or sales total. For Eilish and Rodrigo, that call tied eligibility to the moment of breakthrough, not to the year each first released music.

Ceremony dates add a second layer of confusion. The year attached to a Grammy win refers to the ceremony, not to the release period the award covers. Anyone comparing a winner's history with the rules should match the breakthrough to the eligibility period, not to the date of the show. For Eilish and Rodrigo, that means reading each win against the eligibility year it covered, not the ceremony year attached to the win.

How the Grammys, VMAs and Billboard Music Awards decide who wins

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Free daily briefing on global business news.

Grammys vs. VMAs vs. Billboard Music Awards explained through 10 angles on how peer voting, fan ballots and chart data decide who takes home a trophy

The Grammys, the MTV Video Music Awards and the Billboard Music Awards all honor music, but each one answers a different question. A Grammy reflects what music professionals judge to be excellent. A VMA reflects how a music video fared with a combination of professional voters and fans. A Billboard Music Award, in years when the show tied winners to the charts, reflected what the data measured.

The Grammy Awards are presented by the Recording Academy, a membership organization of music professionals. Its voting members include performers, songwriters, producers and engineers. That makes the Grammy a peer award. The people deciding the outcome work in the same field as the people competing for it.

The MTV Video Music Awards start from a different place. They honor music videos. The competing work is the visual piece built around a song, not the recording alone. MTV has described its voting as a blend of professional and fan input. Fans have voted online in many categories.

The Billboard Music Awards have been tied to Billboard's charts, including the Hot 100 and the Billboard 200. In chart-driven years, winners were based on measured performance such as sales, streaming, radio airplay and touring. Fan voting has also appeared in some categories in some years.

Any comparison of Grammys vs. VMAs vs. Billboard Music Awards comes down to three inputs: peer judgment, chart data and a mix of fan and professional votes. Each method captures something different. A peer vote reflects the judgment of colleagues in the industry. A fan vote reflects the preferences of the people who cast ballots. A chart reflects measured commercial activity.

None of these systems has stood still. The Recording Academy has revised its rules over time, including how nominations are decided. At the VMAs, the fan and professional split, the categories open to fans and the voting windows have varied by year. Billboard has revised its criteria across editions of its awards.

The 10 slides below cover a selection of the mechanics behind each show. They look at who votes, how ballots move through rounds, how eligibility works, what the charts track and what the trophies look like. They are not a complete rulebook for any of the three. They explain the logic that shapes each result.

The Grammy Awards are decided by peer voting. The Recording Academy, a membership organization of music professionals, presents the awards, and its voting members include performers, songwriters, producers and engineers.

That makes a Grammy a judgment by colleagues. A singer may be voting on other singers. A songwriter may be weighing lyrics written by a rival. A recording engineer may be judging the sound of a record they had no part in making.

Peer voting separates the Grammys from awards built on sales figures. A chart-based honor records what happened in the market. It counts how many copies sold, how often a track streamed or how much radio play it received. A peer vote asks something else: what working professionals consider excellent.

The two approaches can lead to different results. A record with modest sales can still win a Grammy if voters rate its craft highly. A commercial hit can leave without a trophy if the voting membership favors other work in its category. Neither result cancels out the other. Each system measures a different thing.

The range of professions inside the voting membership shapes what peer judgment covers. Performers can assess delivery and interpretation. Songwriters can assess composition and lyrics. Producers and engineers can assess arrangement, recording and sound. A Grammy reflects the view of people who work on the creative and technical sides of music, not the view of listeners at large.

Peer voting also keeps the general public out of the decision. Fans do not cast Grammy ballots. Audiences can watch the ceremony and follow the nominations, but the outcome rests with the Academy's voting members.

That design puts membership at the center of the system. A Grammy ballot goes to voting members of the Recording Academy, and the winners in each category come from their choices. The staged rounds that move a recording from submission to trophy and the eligibility rules that decide which releases qualify all operate around that peer vote.

Grammy winners are chosen through a series of stages rather than a single vote. Recordings are entered, screened into categories, voted into nominations and then decided in a final round of balloting by the Recording Academy's voting members.

Entry is the starting point. A recording has to be formally submitted before it can compete. A release alone does not put a song or an album in the running. Every eventual nominee passes through this gate first.

Screening is the second step. Academy members review the submitted recordings and sort them into the categories where they belong. This step is about placement, not a verdict on quality. It still shapes the outcome. A recording placed in the wrong genre field would be measured against work it was never meant to compete with.

The nomination vote comes third. Voting members cast ballots to narrow each category from a large pool of entries to a short list of nominees. This is the first point where member preference decides which recordings advance. Before this stage, submission and category fit determined what stayed in the running.

The final round decides the winners. Members vote again, this time choosing among the nominees in each category. The results are revealed when the awards are handed out. Some are presented during the televised Grammy Awards broadcast. Many others are presented at a separate ceremony held before the telecast.

The ballot is divided into fields. The general field includes Album of the Year, Record of the Year, Song of the Year and Best New Artist, awards open to work from any genre. The remaining awards are grouped into genre fields such as pop, rock, rap and country. There are also craft categories for areas like production and engineering.

Those stages describe the basic shape of the process, not a fixed formula. The number of nominees per category and the structure of the voting rounds have both changed over the years. A figure from one ceremony may not hold for the next.

Each stage applies a different test. Entry depends on a submission. Screening depends on category placement. The two voting rounds depend on the judgment of the Academy's members. A recording has to clear all four stages to win.

A recording can compete for a Grammy only if it was released during that year's eligibility period. The Recording Academy sets that window for each edition of the awards. A release's timing decides which Grammy cycle a song or album enters.

An eligibility period is a fixed span of release dates. Work that comes out inside the span can be entered for that year's awards. Work that comes out after the span closes has to wait for a later cycle. The rule ties each eligible recording to a specific edition of the show.

The start and end points belong to a given year's rules rather than to a permanent calendar. Anyone checking whether a release qualifies needs the rules for that specific year.

The window also defines the field of competition. Each edition measures recordings against others released in the same stretch of time. A new album is not weighed against the full catalog of earlier music.

Release timing is the test for entry. A recording does not qualify through its sales or its chart position. It qualifies by coming out within the window.

That makes eligibility a matter of record rather than opinion. A release date can be checked. Questions of quality are left to the members who vote later.

Eligibility is the gate to the rest of the Grammy process. Only recordings that meet it can be entered. Only entered recordings move on to the voting rounds that follow.

A release that lands just after a window closes does not appear on the current cycle's ballots. It becomes a candidate for the next cycle instead. The result is a gap between the moment a record reaches listeners and the moment it can be honored.

The cutoff can also separate releases that came out close together. Two records issued days apart can fall on opposite sides of the line. In that case they enter different editions and do not meet on the same ballot.

The Recording Academy has revised the Grammy rules over time, and some of those revisions changed how nominations are decided. The nomination process is a set of rules the Academy can rewrite, not a fixed formula.

The Academy presents the Grammys and writes the rules its members vote under. Any change to those rules comes from the same organization that runs the vote.

The nomination stage decides which recordings reach the final ballot in each category. A change to how that stage works can change which names voters see when they pick winners. The final vote can only choose among the nominees in front of it.

Because the rules have been revised, any account of the nomination process belongs to a particular year. A description that fit one edition of the Grammys may not fit another. The number of nominees in a category has also changed over the years.

That makes the rules for a given year the reference point for that year's nominations. Comparing nominees across editions means comparing results produced under rules that were not always the same.

The same caution applies to career totals. An artist's nomination count adds up results from many editions. Those editions did not all use one procedure. The total combines outcomes reached under different rules.

It also applies to category size. A category with fewer nominee slots in one year and more in another gives each entry a different chance of reaching the ballot.

Peer voting remains the core of the Grammy process. The revisions concern the procedure around that vote, including the path a recording takes to become a nominee.

The MTV Video Music Awards honor music videos, not songs or albums on their own. That subject sets the VMAs apart from the Grammys and the Billboard Music Awards, which center on recordings and chart performance.

At the VMAs, a track reaches the ballot through its visual version. The trophy recognizes what appears on screen as well as what plays through the speakers. A nominee is weighed on concept, staging and editing, not on the audio alone. A hit recording paired with a simple clip offers less for voters to weigh on those terms.

That focus changes who stands behind the work. A music video is a production in its own right. It typically involves a director, a cinematographer and an editor. Many also rely on a choreographer, along with designers for sets, costumes and visual effects. The performing artist remains the public face, but the work under review comes from a larger crew.

The VMAs have reflected that through craft categories over the years. These have recognized disciplines such as direction, cinematography, editing, choreography, art direction and visual effects. Category names and lineups have changed from one edition to the next. The exact slate depends on the year.

The top honor follows the same logic. Video of the Year has long served as the show's headline prize. It goes to a video rather than to a single or an album.

The video focus ties the show to the network behind it. MTV built its identity on airing music videos, and the VMAs grew out of that format.

Many nominated videos accompany singles that also circulate as standalone recordings. As a result, the same song can surface at more than one awards show. It can be judged on different terms at each. A VMA win speaks to a video's visual execution. It does not measure how the underlying recording fared with music professionals or on sales and streaming charts.

The MTV Video Music Awards do not leave every decision to a single group of judges. MTV has described VMA voting as a mix of professional input and fan input, with fans casting ballots online in many categories.

That arrangement gives the audience a direct part in the result. Fans who take part are voting on a music video, not on a song or album alone. The video is the work the VMAs exist to honor.

Professional input remains part of the process. MTV has not presented either group as the whole vote. Under its description, fan ballots sit alongside professional judgment rather than replacing it.

The rules behind that mix change. The share of a result that comes from fans has varied from year to year. The list of categories open to fan voting has varied as well. The window during which online ballots stay open has also shifted between editions. A rule that applied at one show may not apply at the next.

That variation affects how a VMA result can be read. Knowing that fans helped decide a trophy does not reveal how much weight their ballots carried. The answer depends on the rules MTV set for that category in that year.

The online ballot sets the VMAs apart from an award decided by industry members alone. An artist with an active following online has a way to affect the outcome. That route does not exist when only professionals vote.

The Billboard Music Awards have tied their winners to Billboard's charts. In the years when results were chart-driven, winners were decided by measured performance, including sales, streaming, radio airplay and touring.

That design changes what a win represents. In a chart-driven year, a Billboard Music Award records what audiences did. It reflects what they bought, played, heard and paid to see. It does not reflect a panel's judgment about artistic quality.

Billboard is a music trade publication, and its charts are central to its work. The awards carry that tracking into a televised event. In those years, the trophy works as a summary of activity the publication already measures.

Each of the four measures captures a different kind of demand. Sales count purchases, whether of physical formats or downloads. Streaming counts plays on streaming services. That measure reflects repeat listening as well as first contact with a song. Radio airplay tracks what stations broadcast. It depends on programming decisions as well as listener demand. Touring reflects ticket buying. It is the only one of the four tied to live performance rather than recordings.

Because the measures differ, an act can lead in one and trail in another. An artist with a large touring business may not top streaming. A song that dominates streaming may not lead radio. Categories built on different data can reward different kinds of commercial success.

Fan voting has also played a part. In some years, certain categories were opened to fan ballots. That added a popularity vote alongside the measured data. A fan-voted category rewards an audience's willingness to cast ballots. It measures something separate from chart performance.

Billboard has revised the criteria across editions. The mix of chart data and fan input has not stayed fixed. A rule that shaped one year's results may not describe another year's. A comparison of winners from different editions depends on the criteria that applied to each one.

The Billboard Hot 100 ranks songs, and the Billboard 200 ranks albums. Billboard publishes both charts every week. They are the measured foundation for chart-based honors at the Billboard Music Awards.

The Hot 100 is a singles chart. As its name says, it ranks 100 songs each week. Its ranking blends several kinds of listening and buying, including sales, radio airplay and streaming. A song can climb by leading in one of those areas or by performing steadily across all of them.

The Billboard 200 ranks 200 albums each week. It counts traditional full-album sales. It also converts streams and individual track sales into album-equivalent units. That conversion lets a project that is mostly streamed sit on the same list as one that sells many physical copies.

The split between songs and albums shapes which artists appear in chart-based categories. An artist with one dominant single can rank high on the Hot 100 while the album it comes from posts a modest run. Another act can sell albums steadily without placing a song near the top of the singles chart. Two separate charts allow both kinds of success to register.

Weekly publication is the other defining trait. Each chart reflects a single tracking week, and positions shift as listening habits change. A song can debut high and fall quickly. Another can rise slowly and hold a spot for months. The charts keep a running record of those movements over time.

Billboard also publishes genre charts alongside its two flagship lists. These cover areas such as country, Latin, rock and R&B/hip-hop. The Billboard Music Awards have included genre categories, such as top country and top Latin honors. Those categories give the narrower charts a role of their own.

The way Billboard counts a stream or a sale has been adjusted over the years. Streaming data, for example, was not part of the charts' earliest formulas. Because of those changes, a chart position from one era does not measure exactly the same thing as a position from another. The exact formula behind any particular year's award finalists depends on the rules in force for that year.

Grammy winners receive a gilded gramophone, and MTV Video Music Awards winners receive the Moon Person, a statue of an astronaut. One trophy depicts a machine for playing sound. The other depicts a person.

A gramophone is an early device for playing recorded sound from a flat disc. A needle followed grooves cut into the spinning disc. A large horn projected the sound into the room. The Grammy statue takes that shape, horn included.

Gilding is a thin layer of gold applied to a surface. On the Grammy, it gives the statue its gold finish.

The gramophone form ties the trophy to the kind of work the Grammys judge. The awards cover recorded music. The statue shows a machine built to play recordings back.

The Moon Person does not depict audio equipment. Its subject is an astronaut, a person trained for space travel. The VMA trophy draws on space travel rather than on the tools used to make or play music.

Neither statue reflects how its winner was chosen. The gramophone points to the medium the Grammys honor, not to the peer vote behind each result. The astronaut points to neither the video craft the VMAs judge nor the mix of fan and professional ballots that decides them.

The difference in shape makes each trophy identifiable on sight. A winner holding a gold gramophone has won a Grammy. A winner holding an astronaut figure has won a VMA.

The Grammys, VMAs and Billboard Music Awards each answer a different question about music. The Grammys turn to music professionals, the VMAs add fans to the vote, and the Billboard awards have leaned on chart data.

The first difference is who holds the decision. At the Grammys, it sits with members of the Recording Academy, the same community that makes the records under review. A Grammy result does not hinge on how many copies a recording sold or how often it streamed. A commercially modest record can win if enough voting members rate it highly. A best-selling one can go home empty-handed.

At the VMAs, the decision is shared. MTV has described its process as a mix of professional and fan input. Fans vote online in many categories. Part of a result can rest on how many members of an artist's audience turn out to cast ballots. The professional side keeps fan turnout from being the only factor.

At the Billboard Music Awards, chart-driven years moved the decision away from ballots and toward measurement. Winners in those editions were based on sales, streaming, radio airplay and touring. Billboard's Hot 100 and Billboard 200 charts serve as the data foundation for these chart-based honors. Fan voting has still appeared in some Billboard categories in some years. The line between measured and voted outcomes has not always been clean.

The competing work differs as well. The Grammys and the Billboard awards center on recordings and the artists behind them. The VMAs center on music videos. That brings visual production into what voters weigh, alongside the song itself.

Each method also leaves something out. Peer voting captures the judgment of insiders, not the size of an audience. Fan voting reflects the people who choose to vote, not everyone who listens. Chart data records commercial activity, not an assessment of craft.

Comparing winners across years runs into the same issue at all three shows. Each has changed its rules over time. A win in one edition was not necessarily decided the same way as a win in another. Each result reflects the rules in force that year. A Grammy from an earlier year may have been decided before a nomination change. A VMA may come from a year with a different fan-vote split. A Billboard award may come from an edition with revised criteria.

Taken together, the three shows can judge one song by separate standards. Its recording can compete for a Grammy. Its video can compete for a VMA. In a chart-driven year, its chart performance can count toward a Billboard award. Each result describes a different part of how that song was made and received.

Bosses can set strict office schedules if they respect staff's family time

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Free daily briefing on global business news.

Many leaders decide alone which days staff come into the office. Fewer people quit when managers also ask when their workday should end

A version of this article originally appeared in Quartz’s Leadership newsletter. Sign up here to get the latest leadership news and insights straight to your inbox.

Some of the country's largest companies have spent the past few years ordering people back to their desks. At the end of 2024, Amazon $AMZN's chief executive, Andy Jassy, told corporate staff to come in five days a week. In the throes of the COVID-19 pandemic, Goldman Sachs $GS CEO David Solomon said working from home was "an aberration that we're going to correct as soon as possible." Many workers protested. But both men were determined to have employees working the same way they had before the pandemic.

Millions of office workers spent more than a year working from home. Many of them stopped working at the same hour every day and stopped answering messages late at night. Many more found meaning in their jobs. Once they were ordered back to the office, though, they had to decide whether they could maintain the same schedule without looking like they were slacking off.

Employees put up with return-to-office mandates if their manager accepts the schedule they built at home, according to Harvard Business School's Grace Mele-Cormier and colleagues. The problem is less about the time they have to come in and more about settling what time people can leave.

Mele-Cormier and his team found that employees tend to quit less often and earn slightly higher performance reviews when they're allowed to maintain the same working schedules as before. But many employees still fear that their boss will think they care less about the job if they go home at the same hour every day. They become less tense once they know their manager is on board, according to HBR's researchers.

The problem is that many managers never tell employees which hours they're expected to work. Their silence on the topic forces staff to assume they have to continue answering emails and messages at all hours of the day and night, just as they had done before the pandemic. Some employees end up leaving their roles for ones that have a clearer schedule.

Executives see the resignations, but they rarely learn that employees quit over their working hours. To executives, some teams seem to lose people for no reason while other teams don't.

Many managers never ask employees what time they need to leave each day. They assume people coming back to the office want one fixed schedule from their boss, with the same start and end time for everyone. If a manager asks each person what hours they need, the team ends up with different schedules for different people, and many managers worry they'll lose control of the team's day.

They also fear their staff will think the boss has no plan for how the team should work. Asking, though, actually leaves a manager with a better plan, according to Dan Cable, a London Business School professor. By hearing employees out, managers can spot the people most likely to quit over their working hours while there is still time to change their schedules. But Cable warns against giving employees decision-making powers. "You get a voice, not a vote," Cable told an audience at the school. "Listening shapes better decisions. It does not remove the leader's responsibility to decide."

Acting on what employees say doesn't always mean giving them what they asked for. That applies in almost every situation. After Jennifer Dulski and her leadership team laid off employees at Change.org, Dulski, then its president and chief operating officer, met with the remaining employees in several countries, according to the Society for Human Resource Management. She couldn't bring back the jobs the company cut. So her team started showing employees the company's finances instead. Once employees saw the numbers, Dulski said, they understood why their leaders had cut staff.

Other leaders change a plan or cover a cost after employees speak up. Geno Cutolo spent his first 90 days as president of staffing firm Adecco North America meeting employees across the business. He then redesigned a reorganization Adecco had already planned, to try to remove many of the frustrations and obstacles employees described to him. "It's important to act on what you hear," Cutolo said.

At Safeguard Global, the HR team heard that European employees were worried about winter heating bills, so the company paid them a monthly stipend through the winter, chief people officer Katherine Loranger said. In each case, employees could point to something that changed because they spoke up.

Employees expect the same proof after a manager asks about their hours. A manager can approve working hours, move team meetings to times everyone can make, or explain why a schedule can't change. If a manager asks and then changes and explains nothing, employees conclude the manager never had any intention to listen in the first place. Employees then stop telling managers what they need to know, and managers can't possibly tell which employees are thinking about leaving until they hand in their notice.
