Can America Build More Housing Without Gutting Historic Protections? Inside the Senate’s Permitting Battle

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

A sweeping permitting reform package moved closer to a final Senate vote Monday, when lawmakers voted 70–27 to limit debate on legislation that could weaken a federal safeguard for historic places.

At issue is Section 106 of the National Historic Preservation Act, which requires federal agencies to consider how projects they fund, approve, or permit might affect historic properties—and consult with those affected before proceeding.

The proposed changes would exempt most projects whose only federal connection is financial assistance, while imposing tighter deadlines and restricting legal challenges.

As the nation confronts a shortage of 4.03 million homes and an aging energy grid, supporters argue that reducing regulatory hurdles will accelerate infrastructure development. But preservation advocates warn the changes could leave communities with less influence over what gets built—and what gets lost.

"Preservation is not a hindrance to progress," Heather Veneziano, director of Tulane University's Historic Preservation Program, told Realtor.com® during a recent walking interview through Bayou St. John, one of New Orleans' oldest neighborhoods.

Rather than keeping neighborhoods frozen in time, she argued, preservation can help communities determine how they evolve.

"Keeping things static is impossible," Veneziano said.

New Orleans provides a particularly salient standpoint from which to understand Veneziano’s perspective.

The city is home to 26 nationally recognized historic districts encompassing more than 45,000 properties and roughly 40% of its urbanized area, according to the city. Indeed, its architectural heritage is so extensive that it became the only entire city included on the World Monuments Fund's Irreplaceable America list.

Veneziano helped lead the nomination, informed in part by the aftermath of Hurricane Katrina and the connections to place that could not be rebuilt elsewhere.

"You lose all your really strong ties and connections to place, but also to people," she said of the communities uprooted after the devastating 2005 superstorm. "And places don't matter without people."

But Veneziano sees the buildings themselves as part of what makes those communities viable.

"It's an economic driver for the community, it's housing at a basic level for the community," she said. "Those are the containers for all of the services that happen in your community."

Along Bayou St. John, Veneziano pointed to the Pitot House, built in 1799, as an example of another kind of value older buildings retain.

Its elevated living quarters reflect a time when residents built knowing water would eventually come, while its surviving materials offer lessons in constructing homes suited to Louisiana's flood risks.

Yet, those lessons might have been erased had preservationists not found a way to accommodate another pressing community need.

In 1964, the religious order that owned the property needed land for a girls' school. Rather than allow its demolition, preservationists raised money to move the house 220 feet down the street on flatbed trucks.

The compromise allowed both the school and the house to continue serving the community. And today, the deal serves as a reminder of how preservation can accommodate development rather than prevent it.

Not every historic property can be relocated, however, and the expense of finding alternatives can test even the most committed to protecting a neighborhood's housing resources.

In March, Jane Place Neighborhood Sustainability Initiative, a community land trust dedicated to permanently affordable housing, sought permission to demolish a century-old shotgun house in Mid-City.

The nonprofit argued that rehabilitation would be prohibitively expensive and higher-density construction would better serve the property.

Preservation authorities disagreed, finding insufficient evidence that the house was beyond repair. Jane Place eventually withdrew its appeal without a finalized replacement development plan.

The spat concerned local demolition rules, not Section 106. But it exposed competing interpretations of how best to protect a neighborhood's housing resources: Preserve an existing home or make room for more?

Veneziano acknowledged that unclear local requirements can frustrate homeowners trying to maintain or renovate their properties.

"If your municipality doesn't have their act together and isn't clear about what their guidelines are," she said, "it can be very challenging to homeowners."

In a city facing an estimated shortage of 44,000 affordable rental units, the case for preserving old buildings can be difficult to reconcile with the urgent need for new homes. But elsewhere in the Big Easy, a long-vacant public building offers another approach.

In September, a century-old firehouse reopened after decades of vacancy, transformed into seven affordable apartments above an early-childhood education center.

The mixed-use redevelopment cost approximately $8 million, including $1.35 million in state historic tax credits and $725,000 in federal disaster recovery funds.

"The Louisiana Avenue Firehouse shows us that demolition of historic buildings is not always the answer," Louisiana Housing Corporation Executive Director Kevin Delahoussaye said.

But the very public investment that helped save the building also triggered Section 106 review—and that additional requirement is what housing advocates argue can make federally assisted developments more expensive and difficult to finance.

Their argument is simple: More reviews take more time. More time costs more money. And combined, that can make a project not worth it.

YIMBY Action, which supports the proposed Section 106 exemption, estimates that at least $10 billion annually in federal housing and community development funding could move faster under the broader permitting package, citing delays that can jeopardize financing or discourage developers from accepting public money altogether.

Veneziano emphasized that preservationists aren't simply trying to "save this fancy house." Their work, she explained, is "really community engagement"—helping residents recognize and protect histories that might otherwise be overlooked.

That concern brought hundreds of preservationists, civil rights advocates, and tribal representatives to the U.S. Capitol on Nov. 9 to oppose changes to Section 106.

"Our sacred places are living places of prayer," Harry Antonio Jr., governor of the Pueblo of Laguna in New Mexico, told the crowd.

The Senate proposal would narrow consideration of certain effects on historic places, including noise and visual impacts, raising concerns about the protection of sacred landscapes, burial grounds, and other culturally significant sites.

Veneziano pointed to a conflict farther upriver in Wallace, LA, where federal scrutiny exposed problems with a developer's assessment of a proposed grain terminal.

There, descendants of enslaved residents opposed the project, which would have been built near historic plantations, burial grounds, and their predominantly Black community.

A ProPublica investigation revealed that an archaeological consultant's original findings about potential harm to historic sites had been stripped from a report prepared for the developer. The Army Corps of Engineers subsequently rejected the assessment as insufficient and demanded further investigation.

In April 2024, the Corps determined that noise, light pollution, and the terminal's industrial appearance would adversely affect Wallace's historic character—precisely the kinds of impacts the Senate proposal would subject to a narrower standard of review.

The company behind the terminal abandoned the project later that year, blaming prolonged permitting delays. Residents celebrated the outcome as a victory.

Such lengthy reviews, however, may be the exception rather than the rule.

An October analysis by Virginia's Department of Historic Resources found that 85% of nearly 9,900 projects completed Section 106 review within 30 days. Just 6% took longer than 90 days, primarily because they required additional surveys or involved potential harm to historic properties.

Those figures aren't necessarily representative nationwide, nor do they establish whether reviews actually delayed construction.

"If you're trying to get a project through, yeah, it's annoying," Veneziano acknowledged.

Still, Section 106 doesn't prohibit development. It requires agencies to consider the consequences and potential alternatives before proceeding.

And as Congress weighs how to accelerate construction, lawmakers will also confront a more fundamental question: Who gets to determine what a community can afford to lose?

Veneziano posed a question of her own: "How do we take the best of what we have, and build upon it into the future?"

Allaire Conte is a senior advice writer covering real estate and personal finance trends. She previously served as deputy editor of home services at CNN Underscored Money and was a lead writer at Orchard, where she simplified complex real estate topics for everyday readers. She holds an MFA in Nonfiction Writing from Columbia University and a BFA in Writing, Literature, and Publishing from Emerson College. When she’s not writing about homeownership hurdles and housing market shifts, she’s biking around Brooklyn or baking cakes for her friends.

stUSDT Begins Wind-Down as JustLend Plans to Remove Its Wrapped Token Market

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Strict editorial policy that focuses on accuracy, relevance, and impartiality

Morbi pretium leo et nisl aliquam mollis. Quisque arcu lorem, ultricies quis pellentesque nec, ullamcorper eu odio.

The stUSDT protocol is winding down, turning attention from the yield its token offered to the process through which holders can recover the underlying USDT. The affected product is a receipt token linked to deposits and off-chain investment activity.

The team’s redemption announcement ended reward accrual on November 14. JustLend subsequently outlined how it plans to remove the wrapped version, wstUSDT, from its lending markets. Together, the notices show why closing a financial product can involve more than switching off its website.

The team says its web redemption service will remain available until May 14, 2027, at 23:59 UTC. Later requests are to be handled through customer support in monthly batches through August 2027. It says the underlying assets remain fully backed.

That is the issuer’s statement about backing and its proposed process. Successful execution will depend on holders being able to complete redemptions and on the assets being available when required. The notice does not explain the commercial reason for the closure.

A long application window also should not be confused with immediate liquidity for every position. Tokens held inside another protocol may first be subject to that platform’s withdrawal, collateral or debt arrangements. The route depends on where the holder’s claim currently sits.

In its November 14 notice, JustLend described a first phase that would disable new supply and borrowing in the wstUSDT market. Existing positions would remain manageable during the transition, with further parameter changes to follow.

The accompanying governance proposal anticipates further proposals around late November covering collateral and reserve factors. These are planned steps, and the announcement should not be read as proof that every change has already been executed.

For a lending market, that sequencing matters. Preventing new exposure stops the position from growing while giving existing participants time to unwind. Later changes to collateral treatment can alter borrowing capacity, making the details of subsequent governance decisions financially relevant.

A 2024 ChainSecurity assessment describes stUSDT as a custodial system in which deposits support off-chain investments. Its wrapped token keeps balances non-rebasing, which helps compatibility with other contracts. The report explicitly excludes off-chain components from its review.

That historical description helps explain the structure; it cannot establish today’s reserves. Reviewing software answers questions about how contracts behave within a defined scope. Establishing that the corresponding investment assets exist and can be realized requires additional evidence.

NewsBTC covered questions about stUSDT’s use in exchange reserves in 2023. That earlier debate provides context for the product’s history, without establishing a reason for the current wind-down.

The expansion of tokenized finance has produced many different sources of return, including the reinsurance-linked products NewsBTC recently examined. Their assets and risks differ, but each needs a clear account of how a holder exits.

For stUSDT, the useful evidence now will be completed redemptions, clear handling of wrapped balances and precise notices from platforms that previously accepted the token. Holders with outstanding borrowing obligations also need the lending-market process to remain understandable throughout the transition.

The shutdown announcement supplies a timetable. The stronger test is whether the protocol and its integrations can follow that timetable while keeping the connection between token balances, underlying claims and actual payments clear.

How to QA a Google Ads Landing Page Before Spending on Traffic

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

A campaign can be set up correctly and still waste spend if the landing page is slow, confusing or broken on mobile.

Landing-page quality assurance (QA) is more than checking whether the page loads. The full journey matters: a person clicks an ad, the page opens, the offer matches the ad, the form works, and the resulting conversion is recorded accurately.

A short pre-launch check can catch problems before they affect campaign data or customer experience.

Read the ad and landing page together. The page should make it easy for visitors to confirm that they have arrived in the right place.

A mismatch can increase abandonment even if the page itself is technically functional.

Do not rely only on a desktop preview. Open the page on a mobile device and complete the main action from start to finish.

Try the page on more than one browser if the campaign audience uses a mix of devices. A page that works in one browser may still have a layout or interaction issue elsewhere.

A page that takes too long to become usable can interrupt the journey from ad click to conversion. Test the page under realistic mobile conditions, not only on a fast office connection.

Tools such as browser developer tools and web performance testing services can help identify the cause. Use results to prioritise fixes; a single score does not explain every user’s experience.

Test the complete form journey, including validation and the confirmation state.

Try both valid and invalid inputs. Confirm that required fields are identified clearly, errors explain what needs to be corrected, and the page does not lose already-entered data unnecessarily.

Use a test record and make sure it is labelled as such so it is not mistaken for a real lead.

A visible thank-you message does not prove that a conversion event was recorded correctly. Use the available tag and analytics debugging tools to check the event during a test submission.

Confirm that the event fires only after the submission succeeds. A tag tied to the submit-button click can count failed submissions as conversions.

Check for duplicate tracking as well. If both a tag manager and a site plugin report the same event, one form submission may be recorded more than once. Compare the event behaviour with the CRM record or another source of truth.

Follow the landing-page URL from the ad through any redirects. Confirm that the final page is the intended destination and that relevant query parameters are preserved where required by the measurement setup.

A redirect can remove parameters, send visitors to the wrong market or create an extra delay. Test the final destination using a clean browser session and document any redirects that are intentional.

Do not put personal or sensitive information in URL parameters. Campaign tracking should use approved, non-sensitive values.

A useful QA pass follows the same path a visitor would take:

At each step, check both the user experience and the technical result. A page may look correct while the form fails in the background, or the form may work while the conversion tag fires too early.

Record the date, device, browser, test input and result. This makes it easier for someone else to reproduce an issue and confirm that a fix worked.

Before directing paid traffic to the page, confirm that:

Landing-page QA cannot guarantee campaign performance, but it can prevent avoidable problems from distorting the results. Check the complete journey before launch, fix the highest-impact issues, and repeat the tests after meaningful changes to the page, form or tracking setup.

Playwright showed a real Buy click arrives with no Referer

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Posted on Nov 17 • Originally published at neuragrowth.co

Playwright gave us the reference case for a bug in our click counting. On 9 November 2026 at 20:09 UTC we drove a real click on a Buy button from the home page, and the request reached nginx with the Referer "-". Every Buy button on the site is rel="nofollow noreferrer", so a genuine click from the site carries no Referer at all.

What made us look: after the earlier per-address limit (P26), the site source still sat at about 200 clicks a day over about 60 products. It was 987 the week before that limit and 1257 the week after. The nginx log for 9 November showed 207 /api/go/ hits from 205 addresses, one or two each. 195 of them carried the Referer "https://neuragrowth.co" and 5 carried "https://neuragrowth.co/".

That was the mismatch. A real Buy click arrives with "-", and these arrived with the site's own address as Referer. An address limit cannot catch that pattern, because each address showed up only once or twice. The record does not say who or what sent them.

The change, P26b, shipped on 9 November 2026 and touched 2 files with 87 lines added and 1 removed. A site click that carries any Referer is now sent on to its destination and not counted. Other networks keep whatever Referer their readers bring, and the forged clicks never reach the address ledger.

If a link carries rel="noreferrer", drive a real click in a browser tool like Playwright and read what your server logs, then treat any traffic that contradicts it as not coming from that button.

Originally published at neuragrowth.co. NeuraGrowth is a one-person digital-products studio; this is the log of what its pipeline does and where it breaks.

Why a polling preview is not a new event

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

A polling trigger can show two rows in a manual preview and emit nothing on its first automatic run. Before treating this as a failed connection, check what the trigger uses as its baseline.

There are three different operations to test:

Those operations can share a read function while producing different outputs. In particular, a manual preview should not silently move the state used by an active polling workflow.

Here is a small JavaScript teaching example. Each row has a stable string ID and a reliable numeric revision supplied by the source. It illustrates comparison only; it is not a complete n8n node or an event-delivery system.

On a first automatic read, the caller establishes the baseline without reporting every existing record as newly created. On a later successful read, it computes changes and stores the new snapshot. An unchanged source should produce no repeated output.

The updated comparison deliberately ignores an ID that was absent from the baseline: a new record and an update to an existing record are different events. Keep the comparison scope explicit. A different table, project or trigger mode needs separate state.

The example assumes a trustworthy revision. If your source lacks one, choose a documented comparison strategy, such as a deterministic fingerprint of the relevant returned fields. Ordinary JSON.stringify output can depend on object key order; normalization matters. Decide which field changes actually count.

A read limited to N records does not prove that the table contains only N records. If the response fills the limit, either complete pagination or fail the poll before replacing the last complete snapshot. Otherwise records that fall outside a partial response can disappear from the comparison and later look new again.

Test a source failure between two successful reads. The failed read should not replace a known baseline with an empty or partial snapshot. Also test a record created and deleted between polls: snapshot comparison may never observe it. This is a reason to choose a durable event log when every intermediate change matters.

Source comparison and downstream delivery have different failure boundaries. A poll may advance its snapshot before a later write or notification fails. Suppressing unchanged source rows therefore does not guarantee exactly-once delivery to a destination.

For a new-record destination, consider an idempotent operation or unique constraint keyed by source, table and stable record ID. For repeated updates, define a revision or payload-change policy; deduplicating only on record ID can discard legitimate later changes. Include timeout-after-success, retry and replay in the acceptance plan.

Use fictional fixtures and keep execution history private. A successful preview verifies a read; it does not verify the whole delivery path.

References: the polling implementation and regression tests used to check these distinctions. The sample above is generic and uses revisions; the linked implementation uses fingerprints of returned data.

For the AppStruct-specific exercise, get the inactive practice workflow and acceptance log. The downloads contain no credentials or private project IDs; connecting requires your own compatible self-hosted instance and AppStruct account.

Disclosure: This article was prepared with AI assistance and checked against the linked source and tests. The publishing account is associated with the maintainers of that example integration. This is a teaching example, not a customer deployment or a claim of n8n verification.

Teacher prep programs probed in Education Department’s latest anti-DEI push

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Let Higher Ed Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The agency said diversification efforts disregard “merit,” but critics say the programs level the playing field for historically excluded candidates.

The U.S. Department of Education opened a string of investigations into teacher preparation programs on Nov. 13, alleging that they discriminate on the basis of race under Title VI or sex under Title IX.

The investigations mark one of the first times the department's Office for Civil Rights has used civil rights protections to challenge diversification efforts in teacher scholarships, hiring, training and recruitment practices.

The programs at the center of the investigations are aimed at recruiting historically underrepresented groups in the teaching profession, such as men and teachers of color.

“Race, national origin, or sex should not decide who will shape America’s next generation, yet many states and universities seem to prioritize recruiting and training ‘minority’ candidates rather than evaluating candidates based solely on merit,” said Assistant Secretary for Civil Rights Kimberly Richey in a Nov. 13 statement.

“Education agencies and institutions that consider race, national origin, or sex as a means to withhold opportunities in selecting, supporting, and preparing teaching candidates violate federal law and compromise education excellence."

Richey concluded that students should be "judged on their hard work and merit, not prejudged by the color of their skin or their sex.”

EdTrust, a nonprofit that advocates for racial and economic equity in American education, said in a statement to K-12 Dive that the Trump administration is using civil rights protections meant to "dismantle discrimination and expand opportunity" to instead "undermine pathways into teaching for people historically excluded from the profession betrays that purpose."

“The administration’s false divide between ‘merit’ and diversity perpetuates the damaging suggestion that teachers of color are less qualified or less deserving," said Eric Duncan, director of Pre-K-12 policy of EdTrust, in the Nov. 16 statement. "It discounts their skills, expertise, and contributions while ignoring the systemic barriers that have denied talented people opportunities for generations. Scholarships that address those barriers help ensure that family wealth does not determine who can become a teacher."

The Education Department's investigations, Duncan said, "risk turning civil rights enforcement into a tool for preserving the very inequities it should confront.”

CUNY, which houses the NYC Men Teach Program being investigated by the department, said in a statement to K-12 Dive that it was "proud of its role educating the teachers of tomorrow and our programs are open to all students regardless of race, gender or national origin."

According to the city's website, the program was created around 2015 in collaboration with the federal Education Department and then-Mayor Bill de Blasio with the goal of putting an additional 1,000 men of color into the city’s teacher pipeline.

A March 2026 evaluation of the program conducted for the NYC mayor's office by Westat, which provides research services for government agencies, shows that the program contributed over 3,100 new male teachers of color to New York City Public Schools between school years 2015–16 and 2023–24.

The American Association of Colleges for Teacher Education said in a Nov. 16 email to K-12 Dive, “Strengthening and diversifying the educator pipeline is essential to addressing persistent teacher shortages and ensuring every PK-12 student has access to well-prepared, effective teachers. Decades of research confirm that a diverse educator workforce benefits all students academically, socially, and emotionally." AACTE added that it does not comment on active federal inquiries involving specific institutions or state agencies.

While the investigations are some of the first of their kind by OCR, they build on previous efforts by the Trump administration to weed out DEI in teacher preparation programs — especially in the wake of the U.S. Supreme Court’s 2023 ruling in Students for Fair Admissions v. Harvard.

Some of the earliest federal grants terminated in 2025 by the administration were under the Supporting Effective Educator Development Grant Program, the Teacher Quality Partnership Program and the Teacher and School Leader Incentive Program, due to what it said was the promotion of "divisive ideologies". The department admitted in a March court filing that it had cancelled 104 out of 109 active TQP and SEED grants.

The loss of over 100 federal teacher training grants last year caused at least 127 teacher candidates to drop out of their programs and another 259 accepted candidates to not enroll after all, according to survey findings released in October.

Those cuts were preceded by an anti-DEI directive that has since been heavily litigated and was most recently blocked in court in September.

That anti-DEI letter specifically called for agency personnel to review all new grant awards and issued grants to ensure those federal dollars “do not fund discriminatory practices — including in the form of DEI.” The directive added that “Illegal DEI policies and practices can violate both the letter and purpose of Federal civil rights law and conflict with the Department’s policy of prioritizing merit, fairness, and excellence in education.”

The Justice Department in August 2025 also warned districts they could be exposed to legal liability by asking job applicants how their “cultural background informs their teaching,” using recruitment strategies targeting candidates from specific geographic areas or racial backgrounds, and asking job candidates to describe how they overcame obstacles.

Since the Supreme Court’s decision in Students for Fair Admissions v. Harvard, civil rights groups have raised concerns that it would set back educational equity efforts even outside of race-conscious admissions. That decision undid race-conscious policies in college admissions, and the Education Department under Trump has used it to justify scaling back other diversity, equity and inclusion initiatives in education.

Scholarship availability, teacher pipelines and student affinity groups were among the top areas beyond college access that advocates were concerned could be impacted in the wake of the ruling.

How is private school choice impacting public school enrollment?

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Education  |  Read Source →

Let K-12 Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

K-12 Dive’s three-part series, Scrambling for Students, explores how districts are trying to stabilize and improve enrollment as the competition for students heats up.

Private school choice policies in states — and, soon, at the federal level — have ramped up in recent years as the pool of students enrolling in public schools continues to shrink.

While declining birth rates are driving most of these enrollment declines and leading to more public school closures, K-12 Dive sought to explore the growing role private school choice plays in these ongoing challenges for public schools through our three-part series — Scrambling for Students.

The three-part series also examines how districts are trying to stabilize and boost enrollment as they face growing competition for students.

Quorum Cyber Completes Acquisition of Ontinue, Advancing Its Vision for Microsoft-First Autonomous Cyber Defense

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Combination Brings Together Agentic AI, Experienced Cyber Defenders and Deep Microsoft Expertise to Deliver Proactive, Continuous and Trusted Cyber Defense

EDINBURGH and ZURICH, Nov. 17, 2026 /PRNewswire/ -- Quorum Cyber, an award-winning Microsoft-centric cybersecurity services provider, today announced that it has completed its acquisition of

Ontinue, a leading provider of AI-powered managed security operations. The transaction brings together two Microsoft-focused cybersecurity companies with complementary capabilities and a shared ambition to help organizations stay ahead of cyber risk.

Quorum Cyber and Ontinue will continue to operate under their existing brands as the integration begins. The focus is to create a broader portfolio and a more complete approach to cyber resilience, while maintaining the trusted service and security outcomes customers rely on today.

Together, the organizations bring deep expertise across managed detection and response, proactive risk reduction, incident response, professional services, and AI-powered security operations. Their shared vision is to move cyber defense beyond responding to threats and toward a model that is proactive, autonomous, trusted and continuously improving.

Looking ahead, Quorum Cyber and Ontinue plan to unveil a unified portfolio in early 2027, bringing together the strengths of both organizations to deliver a broader, more proactive approach to cyber resilience.

For customers, the priority is continuity today and greater value over time. Customers will continue to receive the service and security outcomes they rely on, while gaining access to the broader expertise, innovation and services of the combined organization.

"This is a defining milestone for Quorum Cyber and Ontinue, but more importantly, it marks the beginning of what we build together," said Kevin Hanes, CEO of Quorum Cyber. "While this transaction is an acquisition, our mindset is that of a merger. Both companies have built strong businesses, teams, and customer relationships. Together, we have an opportunity to shape the future of cyber defense, combining agentic AI, experienced defenders and deep Microsoft expertise to keep customers ahead of cyber risk."

Advancing Proactive, Autonomous Cyber Defense

The combination reflects a shared belief that the future of cybersecurity will require more than faster detection and response. As AI accelerates both the speed of attacks and the pace of defense, organizations need a security model that can continuously identify risk, act with greater autonomy and improve over time without sacrificing human judgment, trust or accountability.

Ontinue's Agentic SOC and AI-powered managed security operations complement Quorum Cyber's cyber resilience, threat intelligence, incident response and professional services. These capabilities underpin the companies' mission to deliver a more complete approach to managing and reducing cyber risk.

"Ontinue was built on the belief that security operations needed to fundamentally change for the autonomous AI era, moving beyond alert detection and response to focus more broadly on reducing cyber risk and strengthening resilience," said Moritz Mann, CEO of Ontinue. "Joining Quorum Cyber gives us the opportunity to take that vision much further. We share a Microsoft-first foundation and a belief that agentic AI defends at machine speed with increasing agency and trust while humans make critical judgements and decisions. For our customers, this means preserving what they value today while expanding the capabilities and expertise available to them. For our people, it is an opportunity to apply their expertise to a broader mission and shape how AI-powered cyber defense evolves."

A Shared Commitment to Microsoft Security

The combination brings together two organizations with extensive experience across the Microsoft Security ecosystem.

Quorum Cyber is a Microsoft Solutions Partner for Security, holds all Microsoft Security specializations, is a member of the Microsoft Intelligent Security Association (MISA) and was named the 2025 Microsoft Security MSSP of the Year. Ontinue is also a MISA member and has been repeatedly recognized by Microsoft for its security innovation, including as Microsoft Security Services Innovator of the Year and through multiple Microsoft award finalist recognitions.

Together, the companies will draw on their Microsoft expertise to help customers get greater value from Microsoft Security technologies and evolve their security operations for an era in which both threats and defenses are increasingly powered by AI.

The closing follows Quorum Cyber's September 16, 2026 announcement that it had signed a definitive agreement to acquire Ontinue.

Eterna Growth Partners, a private investment firm and current majority investor of Quorum Cyber is the majority investor of the combined company. Quorum Cyber was advised by Mintz, Macfarlanes, and Schellenberg Wittmer and Ontinue was advised by Raymond James and BÃ¤r & Karrer.

Financial terms of the transaction were not disclosed.

Founded in Edinburgh in 2016, Quorum Cyber is a proactive, threat-led cybersecurity company helping organizations build resilience in an increasingly complex, AI-enabled threat landscape. With customers across North America, the UK and beyond, Quorum Cyber is a Microsoft Solutions Partner for Security, a member of the Microsoft Intelligent Security Association (MISA) and the 2025 Microsoft Security MSSP of the Year. For more information, visit www.quorumcyber.com.

Eterna Growth Partners ("Eterna") is a North American private equity firm specializing in middle-market technology investments. Built on the belief that enduring value comes from enduring partnership, Eterna employs a thematic investment approach and deep sector expertise to support management teams in accelerating growth, fostering innovation and building durable businesses. Eterna invests across vertical solutions, infrastructure technology, cybersecurity, healthcare IT, cloud solutions, and financial technology. Additional information is available at www.eternagrowth.com and LinkedIn.

U.S. 8th Graders Gradually Lost Equivalent of a Year's Learning Since 2013; New Research Implies In-School Cell Phone Use May Account for Half of the Historical Loss

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Strict Cell Phone Bans Using Lockable Pouches Restore 1â2 Weeks of Student Learning Each Year, Harvard Study Shows

First National Analysis of Student-Level Data: Follows 3.3 Million Individual Students From 4,600 Schools

CAMBRIDGE, Mass., Nov. 17, 2026 /PRNewswire/ -- Today, Dr. Tom Kane, Faculty Director of the Center for Education Policy Research (CEPR) at Harvard University, in partnership with researchers at NWEA and Dartmouth, released findings from the first national analysis comparing achievement growth for individual students, before and after strict cell phone bans. Between 2013 and 2024, eighth-grade math and reading achievement fell by roughly one grade equivalent (a gradual loss of approximately 0.01 to 0.02 standard deviations per year), coinciding with the rapid adoption of broadband smart phones. Students were losing the equivalent of 2â3 weeks of learning every year. Although small on an annual basis, the loss accumulated.

Until recently, there has been little direct evidence on how cell phone use may have contributed to these achievement losses. By studying achievement growth after phones were banned, this study suggests that banning in-school cell phone use restores the equivalent of 1â2 weeks of lost learning each year, or about half of the annual decline in achievement since 2013.

The authors analyze data from 3.3 million students attending 4,600 schools nationally during the 2021â22 through 2025â26 school years. The study used NWEA MAP Growth data from districts across 49 states and the District of Columbia to measure math and reading growth among students in grades 3 through 10. The study measures the change in student achievement growth after schools implemented strict cell phone bans, requiring students to store their phones in lockable pouches (Yondr pouches) for the day. The study also provides the first evidence on statewide bans during the 2025â26 school year. Key findings include:

"Around 2013, U.S. students started losing 2â3 weeks of learning each year. It's been a slow leak that, over time, has put U.S. 8th graders almost a year behind. Strict cell phone bans seem to plug about half of that leak," said Tom Kane, Harvard professor and one of the leaders of the study.

"While schools should not expect a large boost immediately, storing phones is a cost-effective way to add 1â2 weeks of instruction each year. That could add up over time, just as the initial declines did," said Douglas Staiger, Professor of Economics at Dartmouth College, a study co-author.

Despite the recent progress in understanding cell phone bans, much less is known about the effect of cell phones and social media outside of school. The recent Meta settlement, in which the company has agreed to limit access for minors outside of school hours, provides a starting point for learning about the consequences of cell phone use after school and at night. To take advantage of that opportunity, we recommend the following steps:

With cooperation from the social media companies, state leaders and researchers have an opportunity not just to track the effect of the Meta settlement restrictions, but to improve them.

"We have lacked the data for understanding the role that cell phones are playing in students' lives. Obviously, the social media companies know how much time students are spending on their platforms, but their data have not been linked to student academic or non-academic outcomes. We have an opportunity to build an evidence base on any negative impacts of social media use on students and to find commonsense regulations for reducing harms," said Harvard Professor Tom Kane.

CEPR received philanthropic support from the Walton Family Foundation and Overdeck Family Foundation to conduct this research.

About the Center for Education Policy Research at Harvard University The Center for Education Policy Research at Harvard University seeks to transform education through quality research and evidence. CEPR and its partners believe all students will learn and thrive when education leaders make decisions using facts and findings, rather than untested assumptions. Learn more at cepr.harvard.edu. Learn more at www.cepr.harvard.edu/cellphones.

About Dartmouth Founded in 1769, Dartmouth is a member of the Ivy League and consistently ranks among the world's greatest academic institutions. Dartmouth has forged a singular identity for combining its deep commitment to outstanding undergraduate liberal arts and graduate education with distinguished research and scholarship in the Arts and Sciences and its four leading graduate schoolsâthe Geisel School of Medicine, the Guarini School of Graduate and Advanced Studies, Thayer School of Engineering, and the Tuck School of Business.

SOURCE Center for Education Policy Research at Harvard University

Chelsea are reportedly going to be one to watch in the race to sign Bournemouth forward Eli Junior Kroupi in the upcoming January transfer window.

The 20-year-old is one of the most exciting young talents in the game right now, having scored a superb 13 Premier League goals last season to fire Bournemouth into Europe.

It now seems inevitable that the Cherries are going to lose Kroupi to a big name, and Simon Phillips reports that Chelsea are keen on him alongside his club teammate Alex Scott.

Chelsea could do with more cover in attack as they’ve struggled while Joao Pedro’s been injured, and Kroupi is a good option due to also being able to play on the left-hand side of a front three.

Still, Arsenal have also been strongly linked with the France international by the Independent and by talkSPORT in recent times, so it remains to be seen where he could end up.

Kroupi could have a big decision to make this January if Arsenal and Chelsea do indeed come in for him, while other top clubs could surely get involved too.

The former Lorient youngster would surely be tempted by Arsenal right now as Mikel Arteta’s side are reigning Premier League champions and favourites to win the title again this year.

There’s also a clear role for Kroupi in this Gunners side as they lack a genuine star name in their front three, with Kroupi surely an upgrade on the likes of Viktor Gyokeres, Kai Havertz, and Christos Tzolis.

Chelsea, meanwhile, could be another good option for Kroupi as they’ve shown they’re ready to trust and develop young players.

The west London giants already have a lot of options in attack, however, so it remains to be seen if he’d definitely play as regularly in Xabi Alonso’s side.

Chelsea are also not currently in Europe and their project has been a lot less convincing in recent times, so Kroupi would probably be more likely to lift silverware at the Emirates Stadium.

This has ignited some fears within the community of the COD-ification of Battlefield.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

A few things come to mind when I think about what the greatest fear of a Battlefield player is. It could be a glint atop the mountains on Liberation Peak, reliving the launch of Battlefield 2042, or maybe it's the randomised recoil from the first Battlefield. All of these are good bets, but I think the real shared nightmare of most Battlefield players is that someday it'll morph into Call of Duty.

To be fair, this fear isn't entirely unfounded. Bryan Beede was previously appointed as Battlefield's general manager after spending 19 years working on Call of Duty and has now been promoted to group general manager at EA. Taking his place is another Call of Duty veteran, Sam Cooper, who worked as Call of Duty's global product management and head of live service.

"I'm excited to share that I've joined Electronic Arts as general manager of Battlefield," Cooper says in a LinkedIn post. "I started my games career at EA on the FIFA team, so coming back to where it all began feels extra special.

"Battlefield is an iconic franchise that has something no other shooter has: all-out warfare, destruction at massive scale, squads play, and a passionate community that has stuck with this franchise for more than two decades."

Now this in and of itself isn't enough to sound any alarm bells for players concerned whether or not Battlefield can stay true to form, especially since Beede was appointed as Battlefield GM back in 2021 prior to Battlefield 6's release. But this alongside some questionable new skins has some Battlefield players worried that Battlefield is becoming more "en-cod-ified".

A big talking point during Battlefield 6's launch was players begging the devs to ignore COD's trend of gimmicky skins and instead stay true to its in-game realism for cosmetics. This has largely been the case—although the Dead Space collab was a near miss. But now some believe the cracks are starting to show with the new Fox Hunter skin pack.

"There's absolutely no way you can defend this," one player says in response to the skin pack. "There's a pseudo furry skin in Battlefield now. The defense I've heard is that 'it's plausible', while these are plausible, the masks are just, no? What tactical purpose does wearing a Japanese baby mask [or] a furry mask serve?" I'd argue, however, that it could stun an enemy Battlefield player long enough for you to secure a kill, and therefore there's at least some tactical purpose to it.

La Germania ha reso omaggio a Rudiger: 86 presenze in nazionale senza il grande trionfo

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Antonio Rudiger ha ricevuto l'ultimo applauso da calciatore della Nazionale tedesca. Il difensore, che aveva annunciato il ritiro dalla Germania dopo il Mondiale disputato negli Stati Uniti, è stato omaggiato all'Olympiastadion di Berlino davanti a oltre 70 mila spettatori prima della sfida di Nations League contro l'Olanda.

Dopo 86 presenze e dieci anni di avventura con la Mannschaft, il centrale classe 1993 ha chiuso un capitolo importante della sua carriera. Un percorso caratterizzato da grande continuità, ma anche dal rimpianto di non aver mai conquistato un grande trofeo con la maglia della Nazionale.

Il debutto di Rüdiger risale al 13 maggio 2014, nel pareggio senza reti contro la Polonia sotto la guida di Joachim Low. Poche settimane dopo arrivò il Mondiale vinto in Brasile, ma il difensore non fece parte della spedizione tedesca. Da quel momento è stato invece protagonista delle successive spedizioni, tutte terminate in modo deludente: presente ai Mondiali del 2018 in Russia, del 2022 in Qatar e all'ultima edizione disputata negli Stati Uniti, oltre agli Europei del 2021 e a quello casalingo del 2024. L'unico successo internazionale con la selezione maggiore resta la Confederations Cup conquistata nel 2017.

La carriera nei club ha avuto tutt'altra dimensione. Dopo le esperienze con Stoccarda, Roma e Chelsea, Rüdiger è diventato un pilastro del Real Madrid, con cui ha conquistato due Champions League. Il suo carattere e alcuni episodi dentro e fuori dal campo hanno spesso generato discussioni, ma nessuno ha mai messo in dubbio personalità e leadership. A Berlino, nella sua città natale, è arrivato il saluto definitivo della Germania al "ragazzo del Bollplatz". Un addio importante per un difensore che lascia la Nazionale senza il trionfo sognato, ma con un ruolo comunque centrale nella storia recente della Mannschaft.

‘We don’t need Gen 8’: Kevin Harvick has the solution to NASCAR’s Gen 7 woes

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Other  |  Read Source →

NASCAR analyst Kevin Harvick has offered his solution to the Cup Series' Gen 7 woes as fans continue to voice their dismay with the Next Gen regulations.

Introduced in 2022, the seventh-generation Cup Series car has been one of the most polarising cars in NASCAR's history.

The quality of racing is at the forefront of backlash regarding the car, with many under the impression that the parity created on the intermediate speedway circuits simply doesn't make up for the stale product on offer at short tracks, road circuits and superspeedways.

While many have called for series officials to now expedite the sport's next generation of cars in order to move on from the Gen 7 cars, Kevin Harvick has another solution in mind – one he has dubbed "Gen 7.5".

Speaking via a recent episode of Kevin Harvick's Happy Hour, the 2014 Cup Series title-winner highlighted how he believes officials will "have to change things on the race car that they don't want to change" in order to alleviate concerns voiced by fans.

Harvick then agreed with co-host Mamba Smith on the opinion that the formula of the O'Reilly Auto Parts Series shouldn't be touched.

"In order to make all this right, Cup has to be great," the Bakersfield-born former driver asserted. He then doubled down, adding: "O'Reilly Series – good ratings; everything's good. Truck racing – everything's good. Worry about the Cup car!

"Don't worry about screwing up the other two. Like, just stop worrying about trying to make that better. It is what it is. It's good for the time being. The racing is good. Leave them alone. If you need to change the body, change the body, but fix the cup car. That is what you have got to concentrate on."

However, a complete overhaul is not what Harvick believes NASCAR need: "We don't need a Gen 8 car; we need a Gen 7.5." He then pointed to two different areas in which officials need to amend things.

The first port of call should be under the hood, according to the 60-time NASCAR race winner: "Just put some more power in the damn car. They've slowly crept it in there, but quit d****** around with putting a little bit in and put a lot in. Like, just make it violent and crazy to watch."

On top of that, Harvick believes the overall structure of the Gen 7 cars needs to be redesigned, and issued a stern warning in the process: "Structure is going to open up a can of worms that's not going to be fun to deal with, but you're going to have to deal with it if you truly want to make it a lot better."

Ultimately, Harvick's assessment boils down to a willingness to tackle Next Gen's most expensive and politically sensitive aspects, from overhauling the underbody to loosening the single-source supplier model so teams can actually work on the cars again.

NASCAR CEO Steve O'Donnell recently poured cold water on the idea of a major car overhaul anytime soon, so it remains to be seen whether officials are ready to have those difficult conversations on how the sport can be improved in the interim.

Nvidia Board Not Considering Jensen Huang's Return: Report

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Nvidia Board Not Considering Jensen Huang's Return: Report

Nvidia's board is not considering bringing back Jensen Huang as CEO, despite a growing campaign for his return led by some of the biggest names in tech and backed by President Donald Trump.

The board is focused on its investigation into Huang and is not in discussions to reinstate him, a person familiar with the matter told CNBC on Monday. The independent investigation, led by law firm Wachtell, Lipton, Rosen & Katz, is examining Huang's conduct and whether it compromised national security or company secrets.

Huang resigned as Nvidia's CEO on Thursday, after 33 years at the helm of the company he co-founded. His departure followed his admission of an affair and reports that he had been romantically involved with Alice Huang, an executive at an Nvidia customer whom U.S. officials suspect of spying for China, as well as with Nvidia employees. The week of revelations saw hundreds of billions of dollars wiped from Nvidia's market value.

The report cuts against a weekend of growing pressure for Huang's return. The Financial Times reported that Huang had discussed a comeback with allies, though Altimeter Capital's Brad Gerstner, one of the investors urging the board to keep "all options" open, told CNBC that Huang had not discussed returning with him. A representative for Huang declined to comment on the speculation.

An online petition calling for Huang's reinstatement had drawn 191,996 signatures by Monday. Its backers include Elon Musk, who said on Friday that he was signing it, as well as Y Combinator CEO Garry Tan and Epic Games CEO Tim Sweeney. The site notes that signatories' affiliations are self-reported and unverified. Nvidia employees have also pushed internally for his return.

Trump said on Monday that he would welcome Huang back. "I would certainly not mind seeing that," he told reporters, adding that national security had not been put at risk, as Decrypt reported. That puts him at odds with Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent, who said on Sunday that Nvidia should look outside the company for its next CEO.

Others in tech have stopped short of backing a return. OpenAI CEO Sam Altman, who was briefly ousted from his own company in 2023, said at a Semafor conference on Monday that Huang had "long been a hero of mine," but that he did not know enough to say whether Huang should come back.

Nvidia shares closed down 4.1% at $156.14 on Monday, and were down a further 0.5% at $155.67 in premarket trading early on Tuesday, according to Yahoo! Finance data.

Nvidia's board said on Friday that it was in the process of selecting a search firm for the CEO role. Chief financial officer Colette Kress, the interim CEO, will lead the company's quarterly earnings call on Tuesday.
